Conclusion\n47\n7\nContributors\n49\n2\n\n\n--- PAGE 3 ---\n1\nINTRODUCTION\nSince the introduction of Sora (OpenAI, 2024) by OpenAI, video generation technology has attracted\nsubstantial attention from both industry and academia, leading to rapid advancements in the field.\nThe emergence of models capable of generating videos that are on par with professionally crafted\ncontent has significantly improved the efficiency of content creation while simultaneously reducing\nthe costs associated with video production. These rapid advancements in video generation technology\nhave also been greatly attributed to the development of the open-source community. Notable projects\nlike HunyuanVideo (Kong et al., 2024), Mochi (GenmoTeam, 2024), and CogVideoX (Yang et al.,\n2025b) have made their video foundation model codes and weights publicly available, gradually\nnarrowing the gap between open-source models and commercial counterparts. However, it is essential\nto acknowledge the persistent gap between these outstanding open-source models and the latest\nclosed-source models. This gap is primarily evident in three aspects: Suboptimal Performance: A\nnotable performance gap remains, as the pace of development in commercial models far exceeds that\nof current open-source models, resulting in significantly superior capabilities. Limited Capabilities:\nMost foundational models are limited to general text-to-video (T2V) tasks, whereas the demands of\nvideo creation are multifaceted. Consequently, basic T2V models are insufficient to address these\ndiverse requirements. Insufficient Efficiency: Despite their impressive performance and scale, these\nmodels often prove impractical for creative teams with limited computational resources, hindering\ntheir accessibility and usability. These challenges collectively impose constraints on the continued\ngrowth and innovation within the open-source community.\nTo address the aforementioned challenges, this report introduces and publicly releases a novel series\nof high-performance foundational video generation models, referred to as Wan, which sets a new\nbenchmark in the field. The core design of Wan is inspired by the success of Diffusion Transformers\n(DiT) (Peebles & Xie, 2023) combined with Flow Matching (Lipman et al., 2022), a framework\nthat has demonstrated the significant performance gains achievable through scaling in both text-\nto-image (T2I) (Esser et al., 2024) and text-to-video (T2V) (Kong et al., 2024) tasks. Within this\narchitectural paradigm, cross-attention is employed to embed text conditions, while the model’s\ndesign is meticulously optimized to ensure computational efficiency and precise text controllability.\nTo further enhance the model’s ability to capture complex dynamics, a full spatio-temporal attention\nmechanism is incorporated. Through extensive experimentation, the model is validated at scale,\nreaching 14 billion parameters. Subsequently, Wan has seen large-scale data comprising billions of\nimages and videos, amounting to O(1) trillions of tokens in total. This extensive training facilitates\nthe emergence of the model’s capabilities, allowing it to demonstrate robust performance across\nmultiple dimensions, such as motion amplitude and quality, visual text generation, camera control,\ninstruction adherence, and stylistic diversity. Building upon the powerful foundational model, we\nhave expanded its capabilities to numerous downstream tasks, including image-to-video generation\n(I2V), instruction-guided video editing (V2V), zero-shot personalized customization, real-time video\ngeneration, and audio generation, among other critical applications. To minimize inference costs, we\nalso introduce a 1.3B model alongside a 14B model for T2V and I2V, both of which support 480p\nresolution and greatly enhance inference efficiency. Remarkably, the 1.3B model requires only 8.19G\nof VRAM, allowing it to run on many consumer-grade GPUs, while its performance exceeds that of\nmany larger open-source models.\nMoreover, we will also publicly present the entire training process, including the large-scale data\nconstruction pipeline, video variational autoencoder (VAE), training strategies, acceleration tech-\nniques, and automated evaluation algorithms, to empower the community in developing specialized\nfoundational video models. In addition, we will provide comprehensive design details and exper-\nimental results, offering insights into phenomena observed during the resource-intensive training\nof large generative models alongside our key findings and conclusions. We are confident that these\ncontributions will play a pivotal role in accelerating the advancement of video generation technology.\n2\nRELATED WORK\nDriven by advances in generative modeling, the landscape of large-scale video models has evolved\nsignificantly, particularly in diffusion-based frameworks. Our review focuses on two broad categories:\nclosed-source models and contributions from the open-source community.\n3\n\n\n--- PAGE 4 ---\nFigure 2: Samples generated by Wan. Wan can produce videos with large motions, high fidelity, and\nrealistic details. It is also the first model to generate both Chinese and English text within videos.\nAdditionally, Wan offers a range of features, including text-to-video, image-to-video, and video\nediting capabilities.\nClosed-source models. Closed-source models are mainly those developed by major technology\ncompanies, which aims at high-quality, professional video generation due to the extensive resources\ninvested. We organize the notable models released over the past year in chronological order. In\nFebruary 2024, OpenAI introduced Sora (OpenAI, 2024) and marked a significant leap in AI-\ngenerated content. In June 2024, Kling (Kuaishou, 2024.06) by Kuaishou and Luma 1.0 (LumaLabs,\n2024.06) by LUMA AI were opened up for public testing, offering powerful video generation\ncapabilities. Meanwhile, Runway unveiled Gen-3 (Runway, 2024.06), building upon Gen-2 (Runway,\n2023), to further elevate the standards in video creation. In July 2024, Shengshu AI released Vidu (Bao\net al., 2024) equipped with self-designed U-ViT (Bao et al., 2023) architecture. In September 2024,\nboth Kling and Luma undergone updates to version 1.5. During the same period, MiniMax introduced\n4\n\n\n--- PAGE 5 ---\nHailuo Video (MiniMax, 2024.09), delivering impressive visual results to the public. In October\n2024, Pika Labs launched Pika 1.5 (PikaLabs, 2024.10), enabling users to customize visual and\nphysical attributes in videos. In addition, Meta introduced Movie Gen (Polyak et al.), a series of\nvideo foundation models that detailed their training processes and applications. In December 2024,\nKling advanced to version 1.6 and, concurrently, Google released Veo 2 (DeepMind, 2024.12) with\nan improved understanding of real-world physics and human movement nuances.\nThese developments underscore the intense global competition in the video generation sector. In\nthis context, our open-source Wan has shown competitive or even superior performance over these\ncommercial models across both internal and external benchmarks, leading in multiple attributes.\nContributions from open-source community. On the other hand, the open-source community\nhas made substantial contributions not only to the holistic video generative models but also to the\nexploration of essential model components. Diffusion-based video generation models typically build\non Stable Diffusion (Rombach et al., 2022) architecture. It mainly involves three critical modules:\nan autoencoder to map the original video into a compact latent space, a text encoder to extract text\nembeddings, and a neural network optimized via the diffusion model to learn the distribution of\nthese video latents. For network structure, U-Net (Ronneberger et al., 2015), initially used in image\ngeneration (Ho et al., 2020; Song et al., 2020), is adapted for video generation by incorporating\ntemporal dimensions. VDM (Ho et al., 2022) extends the 2D U-Net to a 3D version, while another\nparadigm (Zhou et al., 2022; Wang et al., 2023a; Guo et al., 2024b) introduces 1D temporal at-\ntention combined with 2D spatial attention block to reduce computation costs. Notably, Diffusion\nTransformers (DiT (Peebles & Xie, 2023)), which rely solely on transformer blocks, are superior\nto U-Net in visual generation tasks (Chen et al., 2023a). This structure has also been transferred to\nvideo models (Ma et al., 2024), giving rise to two common variants: the original DiT (Peebles &\nXie, 2023; HaCohen et al., 2024) that uses cross-attention for text embeddings and MM-DiT (Gen-\nmoTeam, 2024; Kong et al., 2024), where text embeddings are concatenated to visual embeddings\nfor full-attention processing. Regarding autoencoders, while the early approach (Rombach et al.,\n2022) adopts standard VAE (Kingma, 2013), recent autoencoders like VQ-VAE (Van Den Oord\net al., 2017) and VQGAN (Esser et al., 2021) improve model design for better reconstruction and\ncompression. LTX-Video (HaCohen et al., 2024) modifies the VAE decoder to perform the final\ndenoising step and convert latents into pixels, where the missing high-frequency details are generated\nduring decoding. The text encoder is also critical for text-based video generation. Current powerful\nvideo generation models primarily utilize the T5 series (Raffel et al., 2020) as the main text encoder,\noften in conjunction with CLIP (Radford et al., 2021). In the case of HunyuanVideo (Kong et al.,\n2024), T5 is replaced with a Multimodal Large Language Model (Liu et al., 2023c; Li et al., 2023) to\nachieve more robust alignment between text embeddings and visual features.\nBy integrating these key modules with effective diffusion-based optimization techniques (Ho et al.,\n2020; Song & Ermon, 2019; Lipman et al., 2022), multiple promising open-source video generation\nmodels have emerged (GenmoTeam, 2024; Kong et al., 2024; HaCohen et al., 2024; Zheng et al.,\n2024; Lin et al., 2024; Jin et al., 2024; Yang et al., 2025b). In Wan, we have carefully designed or\nselected each critical component to ensure high-quality video synthesis. We provide detailed design\ninformation and ablation study, which can facilitate the design of future video generation models.\nIn addition, numerous studies have explored downstream tasks in video generation. These tasks\ninclude repainting (AI, 2022; Zhou et al., 2023), editing (Meng et al., 2021; Brooks et al., 2023;\nZhang et al., 2023a; Wang et al., 2023b; Wei et al., 2024b), controllable generation (Zhang et al.,\n2023b; Jiang et al., 2024; Wang et al., 2024c), and frame reference generation (Yang et al., 2025b;\nGuo et al., 2024a), often by leveraging adapter-based and ControlNet-like architectures (Zhang\net al., 2023b; Chu et al., 2023) to incorporate user-specified conditions. We also develop various\ndownstream applications based on Wan, which have demonstrated excellent performance.\n3\nDATA PROCESSING PIPELINE\nHigh-quality data is essential for training large generative models, and an automated data construction\npipeline significantly enhances the efficiency of the training process. In developing our dataset, we\nprioritized three core principles: high quality, high diversity, and substantial scale. Following these\nprinciples, we curated a dataset comprising billions of videos and images. This section offers a\ndetailed introduction to the data construction pipeline employed for Wan.\n5\n\n\n--- PAGE 6 ---\n3.1\nPRE-TRAINING DATA\nWe curated and deduplicated a candidate dataset sourced from both internal copyrighted sources and\npublicly accessible data. In the pre-training stage, our goal is to select high-quality and diverse data\nf\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 36000:68000]\n ten key visual dimensions in video\ngeneration: action, camera angle, camera motion, object category, object color, object counting,\nOCR, scene, style, and event. For each dimension, we randomly sampled 1,000 videos and their\ncorresponding captions generated by our model. We also used Google Gemini 1.5 Pro to generate\ndense video captions for this dataset. We evaluate our captions and Gemini-generated captions by\ncalculating the F1 metrics for each dimension following CAPability (Liu et al., 2025b). The results\nare illustrated in Fig. 4. Our caption performs better in video event, camera angle, camera motion,\nstyle, and object color, while Gemini excels in action, OCR, scene, object category, and object\ncounting.\n4\nMODEL DESIGN AND ACCELERATION\n4.1\nSPATIO-TEMPORAL VARIATIONAL AUTOENCODER\n[1+T, H, W, 3]\nLatent\nspace\n[1+T, H, W, 3]\nDown\nDown\nDown\nUP\nUP\nUP\nSpat.&Temp. 2× \nSpat. 2× \nDecoder\nEncoder\nInput video \nOutput video \n[1+T/4, H/8, W/8, C]\nFigure 5: Our Wan-VAE Framework. Wan-VAE can compress the spatio-temporal dimension of a\nvideo by 4 × 8 × 8 times. The orange rectangles represent 2× spatio-temporal compression, and the\ngreen rectangles represent 2× spatial compression.\nVariational Autoencoders (VAEs) (Wu et al., 2024; Blattmann et al., 2023; OpenAI, 2024) play a\ncrucial role in learning compact latent representations from high-dimensional visual data (especially\nvideos), facilitating scalable and efficient training of generative models, e.g., diffusion models.\nHowever, designing effective VAEs for video generation tasks faces several challenges. First, videos\ninherently possess both spatial and temporal dimensions, requiring the VAE to capture complex\nspatio-temporal dependencies. Second, the inherent high-dimensional nature of video (e.g., multiple\nframes with high-resolution pixels) increases memory consumption and computational costs, making\nit difficult to scale VAEs to long video sequences. Third, ensuring temporal causality (i.e., future\nframes do not influence past frames) is critical for generating realistic and coherent video content, yet\nthis constraint introduces additional architectural complexities.\nTo address these challenges, we propose a novel 3D causal VAE architecture (Wan-VAE) specifically\ndesigned for video generation. We combine multiple strategies (Wu et al., 2024) to improve spatio-\ntemporal compression, reduce memory usage, and ensure temporal causality. These enhancements\nmake Wan-VAE more efficient and scalable and better suited for integration with diffusion-based\ngenerative models such as DiT. Next, we introduce the network design, training details, efficient\ninference, and experimental comparison of our Wan-VAE.\n4.1.1\nMODEL DESIGN\nTo achieve a bidirectional mapping between the high-dimensional pixel space and the low-dimensional\nlatent space, we design a 3D causal VAE as illustrated in Fig. 5. Given an input video V ∈\nR(1+T )×H×W ×3, Wan-VAE compresses its spatio-temporal dimensions to [1 + T/4, H/8, W/8]\nwhile expanding the number of channels C to 16. Specifically, the first frame is only spatially\n10\n\n\n--- PAGE 11 ---\n0\n1\n2\n3\n4\nCausal\nConv3D\n0\n1\n2\nCache \npadding\n…\n…\n(a) Feature cache in our default setting \n(b) Feature cache in 2× Temp. Down\n…\n0\n1\n2\n3\n4\nZero \npadding\nCausal\nConv3D\n0\n1\n2\n3\n4\nCache\npadding\n…\nChunk 0\nChunk 1\nChunk 0\nChunk 1\nFigure 6: Our feature cache mechanism. (a) and (b) show how we use this mechanism in regular\ncausal convolution and temporal downsampling, respectively.\ncompressed to better handle the image data, following MagViT-v2 (Yu et al., 2023). In terms of\narchitecture, we replace all GroupNorm layers (Wu & He, 2018) with RMSNorm layers (Zhang\n& Sennrich, 2019) to preserve temporal causality. This modification enables the use of the feature\ncache mechanism (refer to Sec. 4.1.3), significantly improving the inference efficiency. Furthermore,\nwe halve the input feature channel in the spatial upsampling layer, resulting in a 33% reduction in\nmemory consumption during inference.\nBy carefully tuning the number of base channels further, Wan-VAE achieves a compact model size\nof only 127M parameters. This optimization reduces the encoding time and memory usage, thus\nbenefiting the training of subsequent diffusion transformer models.\n4.1.2\nTRAINING\nWe adopt a three-stage approach to train Wan-VAE. First, we construct a 2D image VAE with the\nsame structure and train it on image data. Then, we inflate the well-trained 2D image VAE into a\n3D causal Wan-VAE to provide an initial spatial compression prior, which drastically improves the\ntraining speed compared to training a video VAE from scratch. At this stage, Wan-VAE is trained on\nthe low-resolution (128×128) and small frame number (5-frame) videos to accelerate convergence\nspeed. The training losses include L1 reconstruction loss, KL loss, and LPIPS perceptual loss, which\nare weighted by coefficients of 3, 3e-6, and 3, respectively. Finally, we fine-tune the model on\nhigh-quality videos with different resolutions and frame numbers, integrating the GAN loss (Esser\net al., 2021) from a 3D discriminator.\n4.1.3\nEFFICIENT INFERENCE\nTo efficiently support the encoding and decoding of arbitrarily long videos (Yang et al., 2025a; Yao\net al., 2021), we implement a feature cache mechanism within the causal convolution module of\nWan-VAE.\nSpecifically, the number of video sequence frames follows the 1 + T input format, so we divide the\nvideo into 1 + T/4 chunks, which is consistent with the number of latent features. When processing\ninput video sequences, the model employs a chunk-wise strategy where each encoding and decoding\noperation handles only the video chunk corresponding to a single latent representation. Based on the\ntemporal compression ratio, the number of frames in each processing chunk is limited to at most 4,\neffectively preventing memory overflow.\nTo ensure temporal continuity between context chunks, our model strategically maintains frame-level\nfeature caches from preceding chunks. These cached features are systematically integrated into the\ncausal convolution computations of subsequent chunks. The specific process is illustrated in Fig. 6,\nwhich demonstrates two typical scenarios in our feature cache mechanism. In Fig. 6 (a), in our default\nsetting, the causal convolution does not change the number of frames, and we need to maintain two\ncached features (convolution kernel size is 3) from historical frames. For the initial chunk, we apply\nzero padding with two dummy frames to initialize the cache buffer. Subsequent chunks systematically\nreuse the last two frames from the preceding chunk as cached features while discarding obsolete\nhistorical data. Fig. 6 (b) presents the scenario involving 2× temporal downsampling (stride is equal\nto 2). Here, the scenario necessitates different cache management: we implement single-frame cache\n11\n\n\n--- PAGE 12 ---\n30\n32\n34\n36\n38\n40\n2\n4\n6\n8\nPSNR\nEfficiency \n(frame/ Latency)\nHunYuan\nVideo\nWan-VAE\nCogVideoX\nSVD\nMochi\nOpen Sora\n Plan\nNote 1: The area of ​the sphere represents the size of the model parameters.\nNote 2: The default compression rate is 4×8×8, except for SVD: 1×8×8, Mochi: 6×8×8, and Step Video: 8×16×16.\nCVVAE\n246M\n215M\n182M\n97M\n239M\n127M\n460M\n499M\nStep Video\nFigure 7: Comparison of video reconstruction performance at 720 × 720 resolution and 25 frames.\npadding exclusively for non-initial chunks to ensure dimensional consistency. This configuration\nguarantees that the output sequence length follows the exact downsampling ratio while maintaining\ncausal relationships across the boundaries of the chunk.\nThis feature cache mechanism not only optimizes memory utilization but also preserves feature\ncoherence across chunk boundaries, thereby supporting stable inference for infinite-length videos.\n4.1.4\nEVALUATION\nQuantitative results. This study conducts a comprehensive evaluation of the performance of various\nstate-of-the-art (SOTA) video VAE models (Kong et al., 2024; Yang et al., 2025b) by analyzing\ntheir Peak Signal-to-Noise Ratio (PSNR) and processing efficiency (frame per unit latency (second)).\nNotably, for fair comparison, most models (Kong et al., 2024; Yang et al., 2025b) adopt the same\ncompression rate and latent feature dimension as our approach, i.e., a compression rate of 4 × 8 × 8\nwith a latent dimension of 16. Open Sora Plan (Lab & etc., 2024) has the same compression ratio\nas ours, but the latent dimension is 4. SVD (Blattmann et al., 2023) employs a compression rate of\n1 × 8 × 8 with a latent dimension of 4, Step Video (Ma et al., 2025) employs a compression rate\nof 8 × 16 × 16 with a latent dimension of 64, and Mochi (GenmoTeam, 2024) uses a compression\nrate of 6 × 8 × 8 with a latent dimension of 12. The evaluation is performed on 200 videos. These\nvideos consist of 25 frames, and the resolution is set to 720 × 720. In Fig. 7, the size of the circles is\npositively correlated with the number of model parameters.\nExperimental results indicate that Wan-VAE exhibits highly competitive performance across both\nmetrics, showcasing a compelling dual advantage of superior video quality and high processing\nefficiency. It is worth noting that under the same hardware environment, our VAE’s reconstruction\nspeed is 2.5 times faster than the existing SOTA method (i.e., HunYuan Video). This speed advantage\nwill be further demonstrated at higher resolutions due to the small size design of our VAE model and\nthe feature cache mechanism.\nThis advancement offers an efficient solution for video reconstruction tasks and video generation\ntraining. These findings not only validate the effectiveness of the proposed model design but also\nprovide valuable insights for the future development of VAE technologies.\nQualitative results. To validate the video reconstruction performance of our method across diverse\nscenarios, Fig. 8 presents visual results for texture, face, text, and high-motion scenes. Compared to\nexisting VAE models, Wan-VAE demonstrates superior performance in these contexts. In the texture\nscene shown in the first row, our method is capable of capturing details more accurately, such as the\ntexture and direction of hair. In the facial scene depicted in the second row, Wan-VAE preserves facial\n12\n\n\n--- PAGE 13 ---\nVideo frames\nGround Truth\nCogVideoX\nOurs\nHunyuan Video\nMochi\nFigure 8: Visualization results of video reconstruction across different scenarios, including texture\n(first row), face (second row), text (third row), and high-motion (fourth row). The above videos have\nrich details but are not used in training.\nWan-\nEncoder\nWan-\nDecoder\nN x DiT Blocks\numT5\nTimestep t\nA panda is holding up a \ncardboard that says \"Wan2.1”.\nDiffusion Process\nCross Attention\nFigure 9: Architecture of the Wan.\nfeatures while reducing blurring and distortion around the lips. In the text scene illustrated in the\nthird row, our method successfully restores textual content with clarity, mitigating issues of character\ndistortion and loss. In the high-motion scene presented in the fourth row, Wan-VAE maintains the\nmotion sharpness of video frames. These results indicate that our method offers significant advantages\nin handling a variety of complex scenarios.\n4.2\nMODEL TRAINING\nIn this section, we will provide a detailed introduction to the architecture design of the foundational\nvideo model, specifically for the text-to-video task, along with the details of the pre-training and\npost-training phases.\nIn Fig. 9, we present the architecture of Wan, which is designed based on the current mainstream\nDiT (Peebles & Xie, 2023) architecture, which typically consists of three primary components:\nWan-VAE, diffusion transformer, and text encoder. For a given video V ∈R(1+T )×H×W ×3, the\n13\n\n\n--- PAGE 14 ---\nencoder of Wan-VAE encodes it from pixel space into the latent space x ∈R1+T/4×H/8×W/8, which\nis then fed into the subsequent diffusion transformer structure.\n4.2.1\nVIDEO DIFFUSION TRANSFORMER\nV-Tokens\nT-Tokens\nLayer Norm\nTimestep\nMLP\nSelf-Attention\nCross-Attention\nLayer Norm\nFFN\nN ×\nLayer Norm\nFigure 10: Transformer block of Wan.\nDiffusion transformer. The diffusion transformer mainly\nconsists of three components: a patchifying module, trans-\nformer blocks, and an unpatchifying module. Within each\nblock, we focus on effectively modeling spatio-temporal\ncontextual relationships and embedding text conditions\nalongside time steps. In the patchifying module, we use\na 3D convolution with a kernel size of (1, 2, 2) and apply\na flattening operation to convert x into a sequence of fea-\ntures with the shape of (B, L, D), where B denotes the\nbatch size, L = (1 + T/4) × H/16 × W/16 represents\nthe sequence length, and D indicates the latent dimension.\nSpecifically, as illustrated in Fig. 10, we employ the cross-\nattention mechanism to embed the input text conditions,\nwhich can ensure the model’s ability to follow instructions\neven under long-context modeling. Additionally, we em-\nploy an MLP with a Linear layer and a SiLU (Elfwing\net al., 2018) layer to process the input time embeddings\nand predict six modulation parameters individually. This\nMLP is shared across all transformer blocks, with each\nblock learning a distinct set of biases. Through extensive\nexperiments, we have demonstrated that this design can reduce the parameter count by approximately\n25% and reveal a significant performance improvement with this approach at the same parameter\nscale.\nText encoder. Wan’s architecture uses the umT5 (Chung et al., 2023) to encode input text. Through\nextensive experiments, we find that umT5 has several advantages in our framework: 1) It possesses\nstrong multilingual encoding capabilities, allowing it to understand both Chinese and English effec-\ntively, as well as input visual text; 2) Under the same conditions, we find that umT5 outperforms\nother unidirectional attention mechanism LLMs in composition; 3) It exhibits superior convergence,\nwith umT5 achieving faster convergence at the same parameter scale. Based on these findings, we\nultimately chose umT5 as our text embedder.\n4.2.2\nPRE-TRAINING\nWe leverage the flow matching framework (Lipman et al., 2022; Esser et al., 2024) to model a unified\ndenoising diffusion process across both image and video domains. We first conduct pre-training on\nlow-resolution images, followed by multi-stage joint optimization of images and videos. The image-\nvideo joint training evolves using progressively upscaled data resolutions and extended temporal\ndurations throughout the stages.\nTraining objective. Flow matching provides a theoretically grounded framework for learning\ncontinuous-time generative processes in diffusion models. It circumvents iterative velocity prediction,\nenabling stable training via ordinary differential equations (ODEs) while maintaining equivalence to\nmaximum likelihood objectives. During training, given an image or video latent x1, a random noise\nx0 ∼N(0, I), and a timestep t ∈[0, 1] sampled from a logit-normal distribution, an intermediate\nlatent xt is obtained as the training input. Following Rectified Flows (RFs) (Esser et al., 2024), xt is\ndefined as a linear interpolation between x0 and x1, i.e.,\nxt = tx1 + (1 −t)x0.\n(1)\nThe groud truth velocity vt is\nvt = dxt\ndt = x1 −x0.\n(2)\nThe model is trained to predict the velocity, thus, the loss function can be formulated as the mean\nsquared error (MSE) between the model output and vt,\n14\n\n\n--- PAGE 15 ---\nL = Ex0,x1,ctxt,t||u(xt, ctxt, t; θ) −vt||2,\n(3)\nwhere ctxt is the umT5 text embedding sequence of 512 tokens long, θ is the model weights, and\nu(xt, ctxt, t; θ) denotes the output velocity predicted by the model.\nImage pre-training. Our experimental analysis reveals two critical challenges in direct joint training\nwith high-resolution images and long-duration video sequences: (1) Extended sequence lengths\n(typically 81 frames for 1280 × 720 video) substantially reduce training throughput. Under fixed\nGPU-hour budgets, this results in insufficient data throughput, thereby impeding model convergence\nrates. (2) Excessive GPU memory consumption forces suboptimal batch sizes, inducing training\ninstability caused by spikes in gradient variance. To mitigate these issues, we initialize the 14B\nmodel training through low-resolution (256 px) text-to-image pre-training, enforcing cross-modal\nsemantic-textual alignment and geometric structure fidelity before progressively introducing high-res\nvideo modalities.\nImage-video joint training. Following large-scale 256 px text-to-image pre-training, we implement\nstaged joint-training with image and video data through a resolution-progressive curriculum. The\ntraining protocol comprises three distinct stages differentiated by spatial resolution areas: (1) In the\nfirst stage, we conduct joint training with 256 px resolution images and 5-second video clips (192 px\nresolution, 16 fps). (2) In the second stage, we initiate spatial resolution scaling by upgrading both\nimage and video resolutions to 480px while maintaining a fixed 5-second video duration. (3) In the\nfinal phase, we escalate spatial resolution to 720px for both images and 5-second video clips.\nPre-training configurations. We employ efficient training at bf16-mixed precision combined with\nthe AdamW (Loshchilov & Hutter, 2017; Kingma & Ba, 2014) optimizer with a weight decay\ncoefficient of 1e−3. The initial learning rate is set to 1e−4, and dynamically reduced triggered by\nplateaus of the FID and CLIP Score metrics.\n4.2.3\nPOST-TRAINING\nIn the post-training stage, we maintain the same model architecture and optimizer configuration from\nthe pre-training stage, initializing the network with the pre-trained checkpoint. We conduct joint\ntraining at resolutions of 480px and 720px using the post-training video dataset detailed in Sec. 3.2.\n4.3\nMODEL SCALING AND TRAINING EFFICIENCY\n4.3.1\nWORKLOAD ANALYSIS\nIn Wan, the computational costs associated with the text encoder and VAE encoder are lower than\nthose of the DiT model, which accounts for more than 85% of the overall computation during training.\nFor the DiT model, the main computational cost is given by the expression L(αbsh2 +βbs2h), where\nL denotes the number of DiT layers, b is the micro batch size, s indicates the sequence length, and\nh denotes the hidden dimension size. Here, α corresponds to the cost of the linear layers and β to\nthat of the attention layer, for the non-causal attention used in Wan, β is 4 for the forward pass and\n8 for the backward pass. Unlike general LLM models, the sequence length s processed by Wan\noften reaches hundreds of thousands or more. Since the computational cost of attention increases\nquadratically with the number of tokens, while the cost associated with the linear layers increases\nonly linearly, the attention mechanism gradually becomes the training bottleneck. In scenarios where\nthe sequence length reaches 1 million, the computation time for attention can account for up to 95%\nof the end-to-end training time.\nDuring the training process, only the DiT model is optimized while the text encoder and the VAE\nencoder remain frozen. Therefore, GPU memory usage is concentrated on training the DiT. The\nGPU memory usage for the DiT can be expressed as γLbsh, where γ depends on the implementation\nof the DiT layers, L denotes the number of DiT layers. Because video tokens, input prompts, and\ntimesteps are taken as model input, the value of γ is generally larger than in ordinary LLM models.\nFor example, while standard LLMs might have a γ of 34 (Korthikanti et al.), the DiT model’s γ can\nexceed 60. Consequently, when the sequence length reaches 1 million tokens and the micro-batch\nsize is 1, the total GPU memory usage for activations in a 14B DiT model can exceed 8 TB.\n15\n\n\n--- PAGE 16 ---\nDP = 4\nFSDP = 32\nRing = 2\nUlysses = 8\nFigure 11: Illustration of DiT parallelism. Assuming a total of 128 GPUs, the innermost layer consists\nof a combination of Ulysses=8 and Ring=2. The outer layer employs FSDP=32, while the outermost\nlayer utilizes DP=4. The global batch size is 8 times that of the micro-batch size.\nIn summary, the primary computational cost in Wan arises from the attention mechanism. While the\ncomputational cost increases quadratically with the sequence length s, the GPU memory usage scales\nonly linearly with s. This provides a valuable reference for guiding subsequent optimization efforts.\n4.3.2\nPARALLELISM STRATEGY\nThe Wan model mainly consists of three modules: VAE, text encoder, and DiT. Following the cache\nmechanism optimization outlined in Sec. 4.1.3, the VAE module exhibits minimal GPU memory\nusage and can seamlessly adopt Data Parallelism (DP). In contrast, the text encoder requires over 20\nGB of GPU memory, necessitating the use of model weight sharding to conserve memory for the\nsubsequent DiT module. To address this, we employ a Data Parallel (DP) approach combined with\nFully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP) (Zhao et al., 2023) strategy. The model parameters, gradients,\nand optimizer states of the DiT will exceed the capacity of a single GPU, with activation storage\nreaching approximately 8 TB in a scenario involving 1M tokens with a batch size of 1. Moreover, the\nDiT portion accounts for a significant proportion of the overall computational workload. Thus, we\nfocus on developing efficient distributed strategies specifically for the DiT module.\nDiT parallel strategy. In light of the DiT model’s modest size, and considering the optimization of\noverlapping computation and communication as well as maximizing the size of data parallelism (DP),\nwe have selected FSDP as our parameter sharding strategy.\nRegarding activations, the input shape of a DiT block is [b, s, h], where the b dimension corresponds\nto data parallelism, s represents the sequence length, and h denotes the hidden dimension. Therefore,\nwe need to examine sharding strategies for the s and h dimensions. Sharding along the s dimension is\nachieved through Context Parallelism (CP), with common methods including Ulysses (Jacobs et al.,\n2023) and Ring Attention (Liu et al., 2023a). Sharding along the h dimension primarily involves\nMegatron’s tensor parallelism (TP) (Shoeybi et al., 2019) combined with sequence parallelism\n(SP) (Korthikanti et al.), which shards the hidden dimension of the activations by splitting the weights.\nSince the communication overhead of CP is smaller than that of (TP + SP), we propose using CP\nto accelerate the batch-level computations while reducing the activation memory footprint on each\nGPU. We have designed a two-dimensional (2D) CP that combines the characteristics of Ulysses and\nRing Attention, similar to USP (Fang & Zhao, 2024). In this design, the outer layer employs Ring\nAttention, and the inner layer leverages Ulysses. This combination mitigates the slow cross-machine\ncommunication inherent in Ulysses and addresses the need for large block sizes after sharding in\nRing Attention, thereby maximizing the overlap between outer-layer communication and inner-layer\ncomputation. In scenarios with a sequence length of 256K and 16 GPUs across 2 machines, the\ncommunication overhead of 2D Context Parallelism is reduced from over 10% with Ulysses to below\n1%.\nThe FSDP group and CP group intersect within the FSDP group, the DP size equals the FSDP size\ndivided by the CP size. After satisfying memory and single-batch latency requirements, we use DP\nfor the scaling. Fig. 11 illustrates the distributed scheme for the DiT module. For example, in a\nconfiguration with 128 GPUs, the CP size is 16, with Ulysses set to 8 and Ring Attention to 2; FSDP\nis set to 32, corresponding to a batch size of 2b; DP is set to 4, resulting in a global batch size of 8b.\nDistributed strategy switching for different modules. During training, different modules utilize\nthe same resources. Specifically, we apply DP to the VAE and Text Encoder, while the DiT module\nemploys a combination of DP and CP. Therefore, when the outputs of the VAE and Text Encoder\nare fed into the DiT module during training, it is necessary to switch distributed strategies to avoid\n16\n\n\n--- PAGE 17 ---\nresource wastage. Specifically, CP requires that devices within the CP group read the same batch\nof data. Yet, if devices in the VAE and Text Encoder sections also read the same data, this leads to\nredundant computations. To eliminate this redundancy, devices within the CP group initially read\ndifferent data. Then, before CP, we perform a loop traversal of size equal to the CP size, sequentially\nbroadcasting the data read by different devices within the CP group to ensure that the inputs within\nCP are identical. In this way, the time proportion of VAE and Text Encoder within a single model\niteration is reduced to 1/CP, thereby enhancing overall performance.\n4.3.3\nMEMORY OPTIMIZATION\nAs introduced, the computational cost in Wan grows quadratically with sequence length s, whereas\nGPU memory usage scales linearly with s. This means that in long sequence scenarios, computation\ntime can eventually exceed the PCIe transfer time required for offloading activations. Specifically,\nthe PCIe transfer time for offloading the activation of one DiT layer can be overlapped with the\ncomputation of just 1 to 3 DiT layers. Compared to gradient checkpointing (GC) strategies (Chen\net al., 2016), activation offloading (Rhu et al., 2016) that allows computation overlap provides an\neffective way to reduce GPU memory usage without sacrificing end-to-end performance. Therefore,\nwe prioritize activation offloading to reduce GPU memory. Since CPU memory is also prone to\nexhaustion in long sequence scenarios, we combine offloading with GC strategies, prioritizing\ngradient checkpointing for layers with high GPU memory-to-computation ratios.\n4.3.4\nCLUSTER RELIABILITY\nBy leveraging Alibaba Cloud’s advanced intelligent scheduling, slow machine detection, and robust\nself-healing capabilities within the training cluster, high stability is ensured. Hardware issues are\nidentified during the job startup phase, ensuring that only healthy nodes are allocated to training tasks.\nThroughout training, any faulty nodes are promptly isolated and repaired, with tasks automatically\nrestarted and training seamlessly resumed. This efficient orchestration effectively combines reliability\nwith high performance, ensuring overall system stability.\n4.4\nINFERENCE\nThe primary objective of inference optimization is to minimize the latency of video generation. Unlike\ntraining, the inference process involves multiple sampling steps, typically around 50. Accordingly,\nwe employ techniques such as quantization and distributed computing to reduce the time required for\neach individual step. Additionally, we leverage the attention similarities between steps to decrease\nthe overall computational load. Furthermore, for classifier-free guidance (CFG) (Ho & Salimans,\n2022), we exploit its inherent similarities to further minimize computational requirements.\n4.4.1\nPARALLEL STRATEGY\nFigure 12: Scaling inference via multiple GPUs.\nAs discussed in Sec. 4.3.2, we utilize Con-\ntext Parallel to reduce the latency of generat-\ning a single video when scaling to multiple\nGPUs. Additionally, for large models like Wan\n14B, we adopt model sharding to mitigate GPU\nmemory constraints. Model Sharding Strategy:\nGiven the long sequence lengths during infer-\nence, FSDP incurs less communication overhead\ncompared to TP and allows for computation\noverlap. Therefore, we adopt the FSDP method\nfor model sharding, consistent with our training\napproach. Context Parallel Strategy: We em-\nploy the same 2D Context Parallelism as that in\nthe training stage, with RingAttention serving\nas the outer loop and Ulysses operating as the\ninner loop. Equipped with 2D Context Parallel\nand FSDP parallel strategy, DiT achieves nearly\nlinear speedup on the Wan 14B model, as shown in the Fig. 12.\n17\n\n\n--- PAGE 18 ---\n4.4.2\nDIFFUSION CACHE\nWe conduct a thorough analysis of the Wan model and identify the following characteristics in its\ninference process:\n• Attention similarity: Within the same DiT block, attention outputs across different sampling\nsteps exhibit significant similarity.\n• CFG similarity: In the later stages of sampling, there is a notable similarity between\nconditional and unconditional DiT outputs.\nThese findings are also highlighted in recent works, such as DiTFastAttn (Yuan et al., 2024b) and\nFasterCache (Lv et al., 2024). We leverage these characteristics to implement diffusion caching to\nreduce the computational workload. In practice, we tailor the diffusion cache to the Wan model to\nensure lossless performance. Specifically, we use the validation set to select certain steps for caching:\n• Attention cache: For the attention component, we perform an attention forward pass every\nfew steps and cache the results, reusing the cached results for the other steps.\n• CFG cache: For the unconditional part, we perform a DiT forward pass every few steps\nand reuse the conditional results for the other steps. Additionally, we apply residual\ncompensation methods similar to FasterCache to prevent the degradation of fine details.\nAfter applying diffusion caching to the Wan 14B text-to-video model, we have improved the inference\nperformance by 1.62×.\n4.4.3\nQUANTIZATION\nFP8 GEMM. We found that using FP8 precision for the GEMM operations, combined with per-tensor\nquantization on the weights and per-token quantization on the activations in the sampling steps, incurs\nminimal performance loss. Thus we apply FP8 quantization to all GEMM operations in the DiT block.\nThe FP8 GEMM delivers twice the performance of BF16 GEMM and achieves a 1.13× speedup in\nthe DiT module.\n8-Bit FlashAttention. Although FlashAttention3 (Shah et al., 2025) achieves high performance,\nits native FP8 implementation suffers from significant quality degradation in video generation, as\nobserved in our experiments. Conversely, SageAttention (Zhang et al., 2024) employs a mixed\nprecision of int8 and fp16 to reduce precision loss, but it does not provide specific adaptations for\nHopper GPUs. As of this report’s publication, subsequent optimizations to SageAttention have\ndemonstrated improved Hopper compatibility. Future work will explore the integration of these\nperformance enhancements.\nWe present our optimizations applied to FA3-FP8 implementation, focusing on both numerical\nstability and computational efficiency.\nAccuracy. To mitigate the numerical errors in attention observed in Wan under FA3-FP8 quantization,\nwe apply two primary techniques:\n• Mixed 8-Bit optimization. The native implementation of FA3 uses FP8 (E4M3) for all\ninput tensors (Q, K, V ). Based on both our analysis of the data distribution and the\nempirical evidence from the SageAttention, we adopt a hybrid quantization strategy: Int8\nfor S = QKT , FP8 for O = PV .\n• FP32 accumulation for cross-block reduction. The native FP8 WGMMA implementation\nemploys 14-bit accumulators, making it prone to overflow during long-sequence processing.\nTo address this limitation, our approach leverages FP8 WGMMA for intra-block PV\nreduction while implementing cross-block accumulation through CUDA cores with Float32\nregisters, a strategy inspired by DeepSeek-V3’s (Liu et al., 2024a) FP8 GEMM methodology.\nPerformance. To maintain kernel performance under mixed INT8-FP8 quantization while enhancing\nthe accumulation precision of O = PV , we employ two techniques:\n• Fuse Float32 accumulation with intra-warpgroup pipelining. FlashAttention3 employs an\nintra-warpgroup pipeline that overlaps WGMMA with softmax and scaling. To mitigate the\n18\n\n\n--- PAGE 19 ---\nUser prompts\nRewritten results\nA Viking warrior wields a\ngreat axe with both hands,\nbattling a mammoth at dusk,\namidst a snowy landscape\nwith snowflakes swirling in\nthe air.\nAn epic battle scene, unfolds as a tall and muscular Viking war-\nrior wields a heavy great axe with both hands, facing off against\na massive mammoth. Experiments are conducted on 1.3B version for rapid evaluation.\nAblation on adaptive normalization layers. Given the substantial parameter load of adaptive\nnormalization layers (Perez et al., 2018) (adaLN) in DiT (Peebles & Xie, 2023), we explore whether\nfocusing on parameter volume in adaLN or increasing the depth of the network layers is more\neffective. To adjust the parameter volume, we follow the setup from PixArt (Chen et al., 2023a) to\nstudy whether to share adaLN or not. In the non-shared configuration, each block’s scale and shift\nparameters are predicted by a block-specific MLP that takes as input the time embedding. Instead,\nthe shared AdaLN configuration (referred to as AdaLN-single in PixArt) computes a single set of\nglobal parameters, which involves predicting scale and shift values, in the first block, which are then\nshared across all blocks. This sharing significantly reduces the number of parameters.\nWe evaluate four configurations: (i) Full-shared-adaLN-1.3B: This is our original model, where\nadaLN is fully shared across all 30 attention blocks. (ii) Half-shared-adaLN-1.5B: AdaLN is shared\nin the first 15 attention blocks while not for the remaining blocks. This results in a 1.5B model with\nan increase of 0.2B parameters. (iii) Full-shared-adaLN-1.5B (extended): AdaLN is shared across all\nattention blocks, but the model depth is increased to 35 layers, keeping the 1.5B parameter count. (iv)\nNon-shared-AdaLN-1.7B: AdaLN is not shared across any blocks, and the model depth remains at\n30 layers, resulting in a 1.7B model. We maintain other parameters unchanged and train the models\nfrom scratch to perform text-to-image task for 200,000 steps (i.e., the first stage of our text-to-video\ntraining), with the global batch size of 1536. For comparison, we measure performance using the\nL2 loss between generated images and real images in the latent space during the training stage, with\nsmaller training loss indicating better convergence.\n24\n\n\n--- PAGE 25 ---\nFigure 16: Training loss curve with different configurations.\nAs presented in Fig. 16, the Full-shared-AdaLN-1.3B model, with fewer parameters, results in\na slightly higher training loss compared to others. When comparing Half-shared-AdaLN-1.5B\nand Full-shared-AdaLN-1.5B, which have the same parameter count, Full-shared-AdaLN-1.5B\nconsistently achieves the lowest training loss. This suggests that focusing on model depth rather than\nthe parameters in AdaLN leads to better performance. Additionally, the Non-shared-AdaLN-1.7B\nmodel, despite having more parameters, does not outperform the Full-shared-AdaLN-1.5B and further\nsupports this idea. Therefore, we adopt the fully shared AdaLN design to effectively reduce parameter\ncount while preserving performance.\nAblation on text encoder.\nWe select three text encoders that can handle bilingual inputs:\numT5\n(Chung et al., 2023) (5.3B), Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct\n(QwenTeam, 2024), and GLM-4-\n9B (TeamGLM et al., 2024). The T5 series has been a popular choice in video generation mod-\nels (GenmoTeam, 2024; Yang et al., 2025b), while the latter two encoders excel in language\nunderstanding, especially among all LLM models under 10B parameters. For this ablation, we keep\nother settings unchanged and train a text-to-image task with a global size of 1536. Text embeddings\nare derived from the second-to-last layers of Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and GLM-4-9B. We measure the\ntraining loss as shown in Fig. 17.\nModel\nVAE\nVAE-D\n10k steps\n42.60\n44.21\n15k steps\n40.55\n41.16\nTable 5: FID scores (↓) of\nVAE and VAE-D.\nCompared to other powerful LLM-based encoders, umT5 demon-\nstrates superior text embedding performance. Following HunyuanVideo’s strat-\negy, we incorporate a bidirectional token refiner as an adapter layer.\nWe apply this setup to both Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and GLM-4-9B,\nbut the training losses and visualizations still show umT5 perform-\ning most favorably. As shown in Table 5, VAE model consistently\nachieves lower FID compared to VAE-D.\n5\nEXTENDED APPLICATIONS\n5.1\nIMAGE-TO-VIDEO GENERATION\nThe image-to-video (I2V) generation task focuses on synthesizing a dynamic video sequence from a\nstatic input image guided by a textual prompt. This approach significantly enhances the controllability\nof video generation by anchoring the output to a specific initial frame, thereby garnering substantial\ninterest within the research community. Existing approaches, such as I2VGen-XL (Zhang et al.,\n2023c), SVD (Blattmann et al., 2023), and CogVideo (Yang et al., 2025b), extend T2V frameworks\nto I2V by channel-wise concatenation of the conditional latent representation with the noise latent.\nBuilding upon this paradigm, our Wan-I2V model adopts a similar strategy, capitalizing on the robust\nprior knowledge embedded in our foundational T2V model to achieve high-quality I2V generation.\n5.1.1\nMODEL DESIGN\nWe introduce an additional condition image as the first frame to control video synthesizing. Specif-\nically, we concatenate the condition image I ∈RC×1×H×W with zero-filled frames along the\ntemporal axis, and these guidance frames Ic ∈RC×T ×H×W are compressed by Wan-VAE into\ncondition latent zc ∈Rc×t×h×w (c = 16 denotes the latent channel, t = 1 + (T −1)/4, h = H/8,\nw = W/8). Additionally, we introduce a binary mask M ∈{0, 1}1×T ×h×w where 1 indicates the\npreserved frame and 0 denotes the frames to be generated. The spatial size of mask M is consistent\nwith condition latent zc, but M shares the same temporal length with the target video, which is then\nrearranged as shape of s × t × h × w where s is temporal stride of Wan-VAE. The noise latent zt,\ncondition latent zc, and rearranged mask m are concatenated along the channel axis and then passed\nthrough the DiT model of Wan. Since the input of the I2V DiT model has more channels than T2V\n(i.e., 2 × c + s v.s. c), an additional projection layer is employed, which is initialized with zero\nvalues. Moreover, we utilize the image encoder of CLIP (Radford et al., 2021) to extract feature\nrepresentations from the condition image, and the extracted features are projected by a three-layer\nmulti-layer perceptron (MLP), which plays a role as global context. The produced global context is\nthen injected into the DiT model via decoupled cross-attention.\nIn practice, the aforementioned approach as illustrate\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 104000:108000]\nduced a unified framework for controllable\nvideo generation and editing, addressing tasks such as reference-to-video generation, video-to-video\nediting, and masked video-to-video editing. Building upon the pre-trained model of Wan, we integrate\nmultiple modalities, including images and videos for editing, references, and masks, into the Video\nCondition Unit (VCU) to support diverse input formats. VACE employs a concept decoupling strategy\nthat enables the model to identify which aspects should be retained and which should be modified,\n30\n\n\n--- PAGE 31 ---\nFigure 21: Unified controllable generation and editing framework. Frames and masks are tokenized\nthrough Concept Decoupling, Context Latent Encode, and Context Embedder. We propose two\ntraining strategies for Wan: Fully Fine-tuning and Context Adapter Tuning.\nthereby distinctly separating visual modality information in editing and reference tasks. We offer two\ntraining modes. The first mode involves training with the VCU as input, fully fine-tuning the entire\nWan model. The second mode utilizes a pluggable Context Adapter structure in a Res-Tuning (Jiang\net al., 2023) manner, allowing support for controllable and editing tasks without modifying the base\nmodel’s weights but with an increased overall model scale. Leveraging the robust video generation\ncapabilities of Wan, this innovative framework demonstrates significant competitiveness in both\nquantitative and qualitative evaluations. It facilitates the compositional expansion of fundamental\ntasks, enabling scenarios such as long video re-rendering. Consequently, the framework offers a\nversatile and efficient solution for video synthesis, opening new avenues for user-driven video content\ncreation and editing.\n5.2.1\nMODEL DESIGN\nThe Video Condition Unit (VCU), proposed in VACE, serves as an input paradigm that unifies\ndiverse input conditions into textual inputs, frame sequences, and mask sequences. It can be formally\nrepresented as follows:\nV = [T; F; M],\n(4)\nwhere T is a text prompt, while F and M are sequences of context video frames {u1, u2, ..., un}\nand masks {m1, m2, ..., mn} respectively. Here, u is in RGB space, normalized to [−1, 1] and m is\nbinary, in which “1”s and “0”s symbolize where to edit or not. F and M are aligned both in spatial\nsize h × w and temporal size n. Under this paradigm, we employ the previously described Wan-VAE\nto tokenize the input video frames and mask information into context tokens. As illustrated in Fig. 21\n(a), these context tokens are then combined with noisy video tokens to fine-tune the Wan model.\nAdditionally, a Context Adapter Tuning strategy is proposed, which allows context tokens to pass\nthrough Context Blocks and be reintegrated into the original DiT blocks. This approach facilitates\nthe seamless integration of contextual information, enhancing the model’s ability to handle diverse\nediting and generation tasks without altering the base model’s weights.\n31\n\n\n--- PAGE 32 ---\nTASK-Outpainting: A large spaceship is flying through space, with a smaller ship visible in the \nbackground. Suddenly, the larger ship explodes in a massive fireball, sending debris flying in all \ndirections. The explosion is intense and bright, with flames and smoke billowing out from the wreckage. \nThe smaller ship remains unharmed and continues to fly away from the scene. …\nTASK-extension: A butterfly with black and orange wings approaches a hanging, brownish seed pod \non a branch. The butterfly lands on the seed pod, causing it to sway slightly, then quickly flies away. The \nbackground is a blurred green, suggesting a forest or garden setting. The lighting is bright and natural, \nindicating daytime. The camera remains stationary, …\nTASK-depth: A pig dressed as a chef is standing in a kitchen, holding a frying pan with a blue flame \nunderneath. The pig is wearing a white chef's hat and apron, and it is stirring shredded yellow food in the \npan. The background shows a modern kitchen with stainless steel applian\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 112000:120000]\nAGE 34 ---\nmulti-conditions\n(a) Wan: Advanced Video Editing\n(b) Wan: Advanced Image Editing\nFigure 24: The visualization results of advanced video/image editing.\nbe removed during decoding. M is directly reshaped and interpolated. After that, Fc, Fk, and M are\nmapped into latent spaces and spatio-temporal aligned with X in the shape of n′ × h′ × w′.\n5.2.2\nDATASETS AND IMPLEMENTATION\nData construction. To develop an all-in-one model, the diversity and complexity of data construction\nmust increase. For controllable video generation and editing tasks, input modalities are expanded\nto include target videos, source videos, local masks, references, and more. Efficient and rapid data\nacquisition for various tasks necessitates maintaining video quality while performing instance-level\nanalysis and understanding. We begin by performing shot slicing on the video data and initially\nfiltering based on resolution, aesthetic score, and motion amplitude. The first frame is then labeled\nusing RAM (Zhang et al., 2023d) and processed with Grounding DINO (Liu et al., 2023d) for\ndetection, enabling secondary filtering of videos with target areas that are either too small or too large.\nAdditionally, we utilize the propagation operation of SAM2 (Ravi et al., 2025) for video segmentation\nto obtain instance-level information throughout the video. Based on the segmentation results, we\nfurther filter instances temporally by calculating the effective frame ratio according to a mask area\nthreshold. The construction for different tasks must be tailored to each task’s specific characteristics.\nFor detailed information, refer to the work (Jiang et al., 2025).\nImplementation details. We train the controllable and editing model, pre-trained on Wan-T2V-14B,\nto support resolutions up to 720p through a multi-stage training process. Initially, we focus on\nfoundational tasks such as inpainting and extension to complement the pre-trained text-to-video\n34\n\n\n--- PAGE 35 ---\nmodels. This phase incorporates masks and develops contextual generation capabilities in both spatial\nand temporal dimensions. Subsequently, we expand the task by transitioning from single-input to\nmultiple-input reference frames and from individual to composite tasks. Finally, we enhance the\nmodel’s quality by fine-tuning it with higher-quality data and longer sequences.\n5.2.3\nEVALUATION\nIn Fig. 22 and Fig. 23, we present the results of the Wan single model across various tasks. It is\nevident that the model achieves a high level of performance in video quality and temporal consis-\ntency. Additionally, we demonstrate new applications resulting from the combination of different\ngenerative capabilities, as illustrated in Fig. 24 (a). Our model performs effectively on these tasks,\nshowcasing significant potential for capability expansion. The unified controllable generation and\nediting framework proposed by VACE is also applicable to image generation and editing, as shown in\nFig. 24 (b).\n5.3\nTEXT-TO-IMAGE GENERATION\nWan is jointly trained on both image and video datasets, equipping it with not only advanced video\ngeneration capabilities but also exceptional image synthesis performance. This dual-training strategy\nfacilitates cross-modal knowledge transfer, creating a highly versatile framework that achieves\npowerful results in both domains. In practice, our model has been trained on an image dataset nearly\nten times larger than its video counterpart, allowing for synergistic benefits between image and video\ngeneration tasks. This extensive training regimen has led to outstanding performance in various\naspects of image synthesis. As demonstrated in Fig. 25, Wan generates high-fidelity images across\ndiverse categories, encompassing artistic text-based visuals, photorealistic portraits, imaginative\ncreative designs, and professional-grade product photography.\n5.4\nVIDEO PERSONALIZATION\nVideo personalization aims to generate videos that maintain consistent identity with the user-provided\nreference (Wei et al., 2024a; Li et al., 2024; Yuan et al., 2025). In this section, we integrate advanced\npersonalization techniques into the video generation pipeline, achieving state-of-the-art performance.\nThe subsequent discussion provides a comprehensive elaboration of our approach, encompassing the\nmodel architecture, personalization data, and experimental validation.\n5.4.1\nMODEL DESIGN\nThe core techniques behind video personalization tackle two primary challenges: (1) the acquisition\nof high-fidelity personalized identities and (2) the seamless integration of these identity features into\nthe video generation pipeline.\nWe start from our Wan-T2V foundation model by first obtaining personalized identity information.\nExisting video personalization approaches usually rely on ID extractors (e.g., ArcFace (Deng et al.,\n2019)) or versatile visual extractors (e.g., CLIP (Radford et al., 2021)) to obtain identity information.\nWhile they make promising progress, their performance is bottlenecked by the limitations of the\nfeature extractors. For instance, ID extractors mainly focus on features for face recognition but may\nnot capture other critical visual cues such as scars or stickers. Moreover, they are susceptible to\nlow-resolution faces, illumination variations, and partial occlusions (such as glasses, masks, or hair).\nOn the other hand, versatile visual extractors are not specifically tailored to the face domain and tend\nto focus on coarse-grained semantic information. Therefore, we choose not to rely on any feature\nextractor to avoid information loss, and our generation process is directly conditioned on the input\nface image in the latent space of Wan-VAE.\nThere are many ways to inject the personalized identity information into the video generation\nprocess. In our experiments, we found that cross-attention operation is suited to interact with\ndense representations obtained from identity extractors, while self-attention is more appropriate for\nmodeling data in the same latent space. Fig. 26 illustrates the design of our video personalization\napproach, which follows a self-attention paradigm. Specifically, in the latent space, we first extend K\nadditional frames prepended to the given video using the segmented face images. These face images\nare extracted from the paired video using face detection and segmentation, and the facial landmarks\n35\n\n\n--- PAGE 36 ---\nFigure 25: Image samples generated from Wan model.\n36\n\n\n--- PAGE 37 ---\nChannel dimension\nCondition\nsignals\nExtended\nvideo\nK extended frames\nVideo frames\nAll-ones mask\nAll-ones mask\nAll-zeros mask\nAll-zeros mask\nAll-zeros mask\nFigure 26: The core design of the video personalization approach in the latent space of Wan-VAE.\nare further aligned to a black canvas that has the same size as the video frame. Then, along the\nchannel dimension of this extended video, we concatenate the face images with all-ones masks for the\nfirst K frames, and blank images with all-zeros masks for the remaining frames. These concatenated\nsignals serve as the conditions. Finally, the diffusion process is performed on this temporally extended\nvideo, conditioned on the channel-wise condition signals in an inpainting fashion. Note that no other\nmodifications are applied to our Wan-T2V model.\nDuring training, we randomly drop a portion of face images in the K extended frames, so as to\nsupport 0 to K reference face(s) video generation. In the inference stage, starting from the pure\nnoise of the extended video, we concatenate the user-provided face images (no more than K) to the\nextended frames in the channel dimension and set the corresponding masks as all-ones. The goal is\nto reconstruct the face images in the first K frames, and synthesize a new personalized video in the\nsubsequent frames that preserves the provided identity.\n5.4.2\nDATASET\nWe curate a collection of personalization data from our T2V foundation model’s training dataset\nthrough careful filtering and automatic data synthesizing. We first filter out about O\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 128000:140000]\nqueue, enabling continuous generation without length\nconstraints.\n• Real-time acceleration. In addition to enabling streaming, we optimize the generation\nspeed to ensure the system meets real-time performance requirements, i.e., the pipeline must\ngenerate new frames fast enough to keep up with real-time playback.\nIn the following sections, we will describe each of these modifications in detail, including their design\nrationale, technical implementation, and empirical performance.\n5.6.2\nSTREAMING VIDEO GENERATION\nTraditional Diffusion Transformer (DiT) (Peebles & Xie, 2023) models are constrained in their\nability to generate videos longer than a few seconds, even when employing significant spatial and\n2Part of this section is introduced in a previously released tech report The Matrix (Feng et al., 2024).\n40\n\n\n--- PAGE 41 ---\nFigure 29: Generated video results guided by camera motion.\ntemporal compression through Variational Autoencoders (VAEs) (Kingma, 2013). This limitation\nstems primarily from the computational and memory-intensive nature of attention mechanisms over\nextended temporal sequences. To overcome this challenge, we introduce Streamer, a novel approach\nthat leverages a sliding temporal window to manage temporal dependencies efficiently, enabling the\ngeneration of long or even infinite-length videos in a streaming fashion.\n41\n\n\n--- PAGE 42 ---\nFigure 30: Generated 15 minutes video with 8 A100 GPUs in real-time 8 FPS.\nShift window denoise process models.\nThe key innovation of Streamer lies in its assumption that\ntemporal dependencies are confined within a limited time window. By focusing attention computations\nwithin this window, Streamer significantly reduces computational overhead while maintaining video\ncontinuity. Specifically, Streamer processes video tokens in a queue, denoising them over multiple\n42\n\n\n--- PAGE 43 ---\nFigure 31: Generated videos with a single RTX 4090 GPU, int8 and TensorRT quantization in\nreal-time 20 FPS.\nnoise levels within the window. After a fixed number of denoising steps, the leftmost token (with\nthe lowest noise level) is dequeued and cached, while a new token with Gaussian noise is appended\nto the rightmost position. This sliding mechanism ensures that the window maintains a consistent\nlength while facilitating the generation of continuous video frames.\nTraining and inference.\nStreamer is fine-tuned from a pre-trained DiT model. During training, a\nsequence of 2w video tokens is sampled, where w is the size of the sliding window (typically set to\nw = T, the number of diffusion solver steps). The first w tokens are used solely for ‘warming up’ the\nmodel and do not contribute to the loss computation. The loss is calculated only on the last w tokens,\nensuring that the model learns to generate coherent video frames within the window. At inference\ntime, the same warmup strategy is employed: the first w tokens are discarded, and the generated\nvideo begins from the (w + 1)-th token.\nEnsuring continuity during training and inference.\nTo maintain smooth transitions between\nconsecutive windows, cached tokens are re-introduced into the token queue at a noise level of 0. This\nallows previously generated tokens to participate in the denoising process of subsequent windows,\nensuring temporal consistency and continuity in the generated video.\nStreamer represents a significant advancement in video generation by adapting DiT models for\nstreaming applications. Its sliding temporal window approach addresses the limitations of traditional\nDiT models, enabling the efficient generation of infinite-length videos while maintaining high quality\nand temporal coherence. This method opens new possibilities for real-time video generation and\nlong-form content creation. We summarize its advantages as follows:\n• Infinite-Length Video Generation: By leveraging a sliding window mechanism, Streamer\ncan generate videos of arbitrary length without incurring prohibitive computational costs;\n• Efficient Attention Computation: Limiting attention computations to a confined temporal\nwindow reduces memory and processing demands;\n43\n\n\n--- PAGE 44 ---\n• Seamless Continuity: The caching and re-introduction of tokens ensure smooth transitions\nbetween windows, preserving video quality over extended durations.\n5.6.3\nCONSISTENCY MODEL DISTILLATION\nAfter successfully extending the DiT model to Streamer for infinite-length video generation, the next\ncritical step is to address the challenge of achieving real-time rendering of the simulated world. While\nStreamer enables the generation of long or even infinite videos by efficiently managing temporal\ndependencies within a sliding window, the computational demands of the diffusion process still\npose a bottleneck for real-time applications. To overcome this, we propose a novel integration\nof Streamer with Consistency Models, a cutting-edge approach for accelerating diffusion-based\ngenerative processes. Specifically, we leverage the Latent Consistency Model (LCM (Luo et al.,\n2023)) and its video version (VideoLCM (Wang et al., 2023c)), which distill the original diffusion\nprocess and class-free guidance into a highly efficient four-step consistency model. This distillation\nprocess significantly reduces the number of sampling steps required for high-quality generation while\nmaintaining the temporal coherence and streaming capabilities inherent to Streamer.\nThe integration of LCM with Streamer is designed to preserve the denoising window mechanism,\nensuring that the sliding temporal window continues to manage dependencies effectively. During\ntraining, we optimize this combined framework to balance efficiency and quality. The result is a\n10 - 20 × acceleration in inference speed, enabling the model to achieve a rendering rate of 8 - 16\nFPS. This dramatic improvement in performance makes the system viable for real-time applications,\nsuch as interactive simulations, live video synthesis, and dynamic content generation in virtual\nenvironments. By combining the strengths of Streamer—its ability to generate infinite-length videos\nwith temporal coherence—and LCM—its efficiency in accelerating the diffusion process—we bridge\nthe gap between high-quality video generation and real-time responsiveness. This advancement not\nonly enhances the practicality of diffusion models for streaming applications but also opens up new\npossibilities for immersive, interactive, and real-time media creation.\nQuantization for running in customer-level devices.\nWhile the generating speed has been accel-\nerated to real-time levels, deploying the model on consumer-level devices remains challenging due to\nthe high computational and memory requirements, even for high-end GPUs like the NVIDIA 4090.\nTo address this, we introduce quantization techniques to optimize the model for efficient deployment.\nSpecifically, we employ two distinct quantization strategies: int8 quantization (torchao maintainers &\ncontributors, 2024) for attention layers and linear heads, and TensorRT quantization (NVIDIA, 2023)\nfor the overall model. The int8 quantization approach significantly reduces memory consumption by\nconverting weights and activations to 8-bit integers, while maintaining the overall generation quality.\nHowever, this method provides minimal acceleration in generating speed, as it primarily focuses on\nmemory efficiency rather than computational optimization. On the other hand, TensorRT quantization\noffers a more comprehensive solution, enabling substantial acceleration in generation speed. This\ntechnique allows the model to achieve real-time performance of 8 FPS even on a single 4090 GPU,\nmaking it feasible for consumer-level devices. However, TensorRT quantization introduces a modest\nincrease in network error, which can be observed even when its built-in error-checking mechanisms\nare enabled. This error manifests as slight deviations in output quality, such as minor artifacts or\ninconsistencies in the generated video. In some cases, it may also lead to measurable instability, such\nas flickering or temporal incoherence. Despite these trade-offs, the combination of int8 and TensorRT\nquantization provides a balanced approach to optimizing the model for real-time, consumer-level\ndeployment, ensuring both efficiency and practicality while maintaining acceptable generation quality.\nBy carefully tuning the quantization parameters and leveraging TensorRT’s error-checking features,\nwe mitigate these issues to the greatest extent possible, enabling robust and efficient performance on\nconsumer-grade hardware.\n5.7\nAUDIO GENERATION\nThe primary objective of audio generation in this work is to produce synchronized soundtracks for\nvideo clips, formulated as a video-to-audio (V2A) generation framework. The generated soundtracks\nconsist of ambient sound and background music, explicitly excluding speech or vocal elements.\nIn contrast to text-to-audio models (Liu et al., 2023b; 2024c), the ambient sound generated by\nvideo-to-audio models must be temporally aligned with the visual content of the video, while the\n44\n\n\n--- PAGE 45 ---\nOutput waveform\nDiT blocks × N\numT5\n……\n1D-VAE\n1D-VAE\nDecoder\nEncoder\nThe video description: a horse \nis running. This audio \ncontains ambient sound: the \nsound of clip-clop. This audio \ndoes not contain music.\nAudio waveform\nConditioning\nCLIP\nAdd noise\nTemporal Alignment \nFigure 32: Framework of video-to-audio generation. Our model processes both a video chunk and its\nassociated textual description as dual inputs to synthesize high-quality, semantically coherent audio.\naccompanying music should accurately reflect the emotional tone and contextual setting of the\nvideo, as would naturally be expected. Furthermore, to enhance user control over sound design,\nour framework integrates both video and textual prompts, enabling users to specify on-screen or\noff-screen sounds and define the style and presence of background music.\n5.7.1\nMODEL DESIGN\nConsistent with our video generation framework, our video-to-audio model also utilizes a diffusion\ntransformer (DiT) (Peebles & Xie, 2023) based on the flow-matching (Lipman et al., 2022) to model\nthe denoising diffusion process in the audio domain. The detailed pipeline of our V2A model is\nillustrated in Fig. 32.\nAudio autoencoder. A widely adopted audio compression approach involves transforming raw\nwaveforms into mel-spectrograms, followed by encoding them into Ha × W a × Ca latent features\nusing an image-based variational autoencoder, as demonstrated in (Liu et al., 2023b; 2024c). However,\nin the context of a diffusion transformer backbone, these latent features must be partitioned into\npatches and reshaped into a tensor of size (Ha · W a) × Ca. This patching-and-reshaping process can\ndisrupt the temporal alignment with the corresponding video content, posing a significant challenge\nfor synchronization. Considering this limitation, we train a compression model that operates directly\non raw waveforms. Our 1D-VAE generates latent features of size T a × Ca, where T a represents the\nsequence length along the time axis, preserving explicit temporal information essential for accurate\nsynchronization.\nVideo and text encoder. To achieve frame-accurate synchronization between synthesized audio\nand visual sequences, we establish temporal coherence through multimodal feature fusion. Our\narchitecture first extracts frame-wise visual embeddings using a CLIP Radford et al. (2021) model,\nthen performs temporal rate adaptation through feature replication to match the audio feature’s\nsampling rate, following (Polyak et al.). The synchronized visual and acoustic features undergo\ndimension transformation via linear projection layers to align with DiT’s latent space, followed by\nelement-wise summation for multimodal fusion. For linguistic understanding across languages, we\nleverage the pre-trained umT5 model (Chung et al., 2023) as our text encoder, capitalizing on its\ncross-lingual representation capabilities through frozen parameters.\nData.
